[PMID: ] Beyond reflective verbal self-reports: Development and validation of the Meditation Pictographic Inventory (MPI) through a multi-source framework

<p>Single-item measures are increasingly used to capture constructs that are impractical to assess with multi-item scales, yet their reliability is difficult to estimate and validity evidence is rarely combined across sources. Meditation-related states, which are embodied and often hard to verbalize, illustrate this challenge. The aim of this study is to present a multi-source framework and apply it for developing and validating a Meditation Pictographic Inventory (MPI), a 23-item inventory of single-item measures to assess meditation-related states. The study followed two phases. Phase 1 aimed to develop and refine the pictographic items through qualitative interviews with 10 meditation experts. Phase 2 aimed to validate the MPI. Adults (N = 378) from the general population were randomized to one of four conditions: three brief meditation practices (counting breath, loving-kindness, decentering) or an active control. Validity evidence covered content, response processes, sensitivity to change, relations with other variables, incremental prediction, and internal structure via network analysis at pre- and post-meditation. Reliability was examined through test-retest, criterion-based, and generalizability analyses. Results showed that MPI items clustered into positive, negative, and heterogeneous experiences, with a moderately stable structure and no detectable structural change from pre- to post-meditation. However, nine items were sensitive to meditation effects, including both general (e.g., body boundaries) and practice-specific effects (e.g., warmth after loving-kindness). Items correlated with related self-reports as expected and predicted post-meditation outcomes, but not beyond baseline levels. Single-item reliability was modest. Positive and negative cluster scores showed adequate generalizability, whereas time- and self-related items require further revision. The study was preregistered, the MPI and all materials are openly available, and the framework offers a template for validating other single item inventories.</p>

[PMID: 42891832] Beyond psychedelic exceptionalism: how people combine meditation with psychoactive substances, and how this relates to well-being.

<h4>Background</h4>Combining meditation with psychoactive substances is often assumed to be a niche practice centred on classic psychedelics. We sought to broaden this narrative in two cross-sectional studies.<h4>Methods</h4>Study 1 used a broad naturalistic sample (N = 1,391) to examine the prevalence of substance use, meditation, and their simultaneous combination, as well as associations with subjective happiness. Study 2 used a stratified subsample (n = 397) focusing on classic psychedelic and cannabis users, the most common psychedelic-type substances combined with meditation, to assess associations with mental well-being, peace of mind, psychological flexibility, depression, and anxiety.<h4>Results</h4>Contrary to prevailing assumptions, alcohol (29%), cannabis (19%), and nicotine (16%) were far more commonly combined with meditation than classic psychedelics (6%). Classic psychedelic use was also embedded within a pattern of pronounced polysubstance use, suggesting that it may reflect a broader substance-use profile rather than an isolated behavioural practice. Regression analyses found no consistent associations between psychedelic or cannabis use, with or without meditation, and any outcome. However, an unexpected pattern emerged for other drugs and alcohol. Other- drug use without meditation was associated with poorer well-being outcomes relative to no other-drug use, whereas other-drug use combined with meditation was not. Alcohol combined with meditation was associated with better well-being outcomes relative to alcohol use without meditation.<h4>Conclusions</h4>These findings suggest that combining meditation with substance use is not limited to psychedelics and should be studied within broader patterns of polysubstance use and meditation practice.

[PMID: ] Facilitating Meditation with Focused Ultrasound Neuromodulation: A Sham-Controlled Crossover Study in Long-Term and Novice Meditators

<p>Sustained contemplative practice confers well-documented psychological and physiological benefits, yet the experiential deepening that motivates practitioners can require many hours of practice, and mastery is not assured. Methods that accelerate training would broaden access to these benefits and could also test mechanism: the posterior cingulate cortex (PCC), caudate, and anterior insula are implicated in meditation, but only correlationally. We used low-intensity transcranial focused ultrasound (FUS) to ask whether reversibly perturbing each region shifts meditative experience in real time. In a single-blind, sham-controlled, counterbalanced crossover design, long-term practitioners of insight meditation in the S.N. Goenka Vipassana tradition (LTMs; n = 36) and meditation-naïve novices (n = 12; an exploratory cohort; meditation experience not randomized) received 12 minutes of inhibition-biased (5% duty cycle) FUS to the PCC, bilateral caudate, or bilateral anterior insula, or sham, in separate sessions during hour-long meditation. Every ~4 minutes, participants rated meditative depth (anchored from “extremely shallow” to “extremely deep”) and intensity (relative to their normal experience, along any dimension), with autonomic, mood, and trait measures as secondary outcomes. Sham-relative changes in rating trajectories were tested with generalized linear mixed models and false discovery rate (FDR)-corrected contrasts. In LTMs, caudate stimulation produced a significantly steeper rise in depth than sham and insula stimulation; in novices, PCC stimulation produced a significantly steeper rise in depth than sham, caudate, and insula stimulation, such that the caudate-versus-PCC difference in depth slopes differed significantly between groups, reversing in sign. No target altered intensity relative to sham, insula stimulation had no sham-relative effect on meditation, and post-session mood and trait measures were unchanged. Autonomic effects were modest and did not account for the meditative effects, and expectation of benefit did not differ across conditions in LTMs. FUS neuromodulation can causally and region-specifically accelerate meditative deepening, with the effective target differing by meditation experience (caudate in LTMs, PCC in novices), a dependence that may reflect practice itself or its correlates. Tempered by the single-blind protocol, the small novice cohort, and effects extending beyond sonication, these findings establish proof-of-principle for neuromodulatory facilitation of meditation and motivate individualized, closed-loop applications.</p>

[PMID: 42853624] Neural Oscillatory and Sympathetic Arousal Dynamics During Mindfulness Meditation: Characterizing Relaxed Alertness.

Mindfulness meditation is often characterized as a state of relaxed alertness, yet the neural and autonomic processes that define this state remain unclear. This study examined concurrent electroencephalographic (EEG) oscillations and skin conductance level (SCL) during brief breath-focused mindfulness meditation, with a focus on temporal dynamics. EEG and SCL were recorded during 2 min of eyes-closed rest and a 15 min mindfulness meditation, with analyses focused on 8 min of the silent self-guided period following instruction, divided into four consecutive 2 min segments. Planned contrasts compared eyes-closed rest with mindfulness meditation and examined changes across the meditation period, while sex-related variability was explored. Meditation was associated with greater theta (4-7.5 Hz) amplitude than rest. Exploratory analyses also indicated greater delta (1-3.5 Hz) amplitude during meditation and a progressive increase in delta across the meditation period. Alpha amplitude (8-13 Hz) showed no reliable state-related or temporal effects. SCL did not differ between rest and meditation overall, although an exploratory trend analysis indicated an initial decrease followed by a later increase during meditation. Global alpha amplitude was positively associated with SCL during meditation, after accounting for participant differences and changes across the meditation period. Exploratory sex analyses suggested a larger rest-to-meditation theta increase in males and greater beta and gamma amplitudes in females across states, although these findings should be considered preliminary. Together, these results suggest that mindfulness meditation is not simply characterized by reduced arousal, but instead involves dynamic patterns of neural activity and sympathetic arousal that are broadly consistent with relaxed alertness.

[PMID: ] Effects of an 18-month meditation training on subjective and objective sleep quality in older adults: a secondary analysis of the Age-Well randomized controlled trial

<h4>Study objectives</h4>  Aging is associated with increased sleep complaints and changes in sleep architecture, microstructure and rest-activity rhythms. Meditation-based interventions have shown benefits on subjective sleep quality, but their effects on objective sleep parameters remain largely underexplored. This study investigated the effects of an 18-month meditation intervention on sleep combining subjective (questionnaires) and objective (polysomnography and actigraphy) measures in older adults. <h4>Methods</h4>  A total of 120 cognitively unimpaired older adults (≥65 y) with minimal sleep complaint completed questionnaires, one week of actigraphy and in-home polysomnography at baseline, and again 18 months later. Following the baseline visit, participants were randomly assigned to one of 3 groups: meditation training, non-native language training (active control), or passive control. Group-by-visit interactions were examined using mixed-effects models for sleep questionnaires, actigraphy-derived metrics, sleep architecture and microstructure, including slow waves, spindles, and spectral power. <h4>Results</h4>  Compared to the passive control group only, meditation training was associated with greater ease of morning awakening (p=0.04). No significant intervention effects were observed for any other sleep parameter, including actigraphy- and polysomnography-derived measures. These findings remained unchanged after adjustment for depressive symptoms and trait anxiety. <h4>Conclusion</h4>  In cognitively unimpaired older adults, this 18-month meditation intervention yielded only limited improvements in subjective sleep assessment. Meditation may therefore have subtle effects on self-reported sleep quality in this healthy older population, although no effects on objective sleep measures were detected. Future studies should examine whether adapting the content, intensity, or timing of meditation practice and enrolling populations with clinically meaningful sleep disturbances or psychoaffective symptoms may yield greater benefits. <h4>Statement of significance</h4>  Sleep quality is impaired with age and may contribute to cognitive decline. This randomized controlled trial is the first to assess the effects of an 18-month meditation intervention on both subjective and objective sleep parameters in cognitively unimpaired older adults, compared with active and passive control groups. Meditation improved perceived ease of morning awakening compared to the passive control group, but no other group differences were observed. These findings suggest that meditation, an accessible and low-cost intervention, may modestly improve some aspects of self-reported sleep quality, while having limited effects on objective sleep measures in older adults without sleep complaints. Studies evaluating the effects of meditation on objective sleep measures in more diverse and clinically affected populations are needed. <h4>Graphical abstract</h4>

[PMID: ] Adult development, meditation, wisdom, and unlearning: Toward a science of advanced meditation

<p>Contemplative science is entering a “third wave” that investigates advanced meditative development, including the study of advanced states and practitioners and the phenomenology and neurocognitive correlates of enduring trait transformations (meditative development). This paper argues that these research directions would benefit from the systematic inclusion of adult cognitive-developmental theory. However, we identify structural limitations in current developmental models that privilege hierarchical, ascending growth while overlooking complementary dynamics of release or letting go emphasized in contemplative theory, wisdom traditions, and psychotherapy. We propose an expanded developmental framework that balances ascending integration with descending or apophatic processes of deconstruction or surrender. This bidirectional model illuminates persistent confusions in the field—such as distinctions between state and stage, characteristics of late-stage achievements (toward “awakening”), how spiritual adepts can be unethical, and potential adverse effects of meditation or premature transformation. By integrating contemplative science with developmental and psychological theory, we aim to provide a more complete account of human cognitive and spiritual maturation that adds conceptual tools to clarify empirical research questions, psychological assessments, and intervention design.</p>

[PMID: 42809248] Understanding the psycho-neuro-endocrine-immunology pathways in glaucoma and their modulation by mindfulness meditation.

Glaucoma is among the leading causes of irreversible blindness worldwide. It is increasingly being recognized as a complex and multifactorial disease with diverse pathogenesis. Elevated intraocular pressure (IOP) remains the primary modifiable risk factor and continues to be the cornerstone of current glaucoma management. The accumulating evidence provided clinical insights that factor beyond IOP may also influence disease onset and progression. In the recent era, chronic stress has emerged out to be one such factor and has been linked to alteration in neuroendocrine, autonomic, and inflammatory pathways. The evolving concept of Psycho-Neuro-Endocrine-Immunology (PNEI) provides an integrative framework to examine these interactions and their possible relevance to glaucoma pathophysiology. The narrative review was carried out on existing evidence that relates biology of stress to glaucoma within the framework of "PNEI" and "Allostatic load." In addition, it will also explore the potential role of mindfulness meditation to serve as a nonpharmacological adjunct capable of modulating key PNEI pathways, which include cortisol regulation, autonomic balance, neuroinflammation, and oxidative stress. The evidence available to date is encouraging regarding the effects of mindfulness-based interventions. However, the current literature is limited by considerable variability in study designs, intervention protocol, outcome measures, and duration of follow-up. Larger prospective studies with standardized intervention and longer follow-up are needed to clarify the role of mindfulness-based intervention in glaucoma care. Integration of PNEI-based approaches may offer a conceptual framework for future research in holistic glaucoma care, pending validation of long-term clinical outcomes.

[PMID: ] App-based daily meditation and menstrual cycle-related effects: a quantitative one-group pilot study

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>  <bold>Objectives</bold>  The menstrual cycle has multifaceted influence. Potential effects on the adoption of healthy behaviors are unclear. Therefore, we examined how cycle phases are related to daily meditation duration in novices. We assessed associations between daily app-based meditation, menstruation cycle and health-related outcomes in meditation novices.  <bold>Methods</bold>  This eight-week study captured two consecutive menstrual cycles. Participants meditated using the 7mind app and reported basal temperature, onset of menstruation, health, motivation, and effort daily. Generalized linear mixed models (GLMM) and linear mixed models (LMM) analyzed cycle and health effects on meditation duration. Wilcoxon signed-rank tests assessed pre-post-effects. Correlation analyses examined associations between meditation duration and outcomes.  <bold>Result</bold>  41 participants (mean age 29.85 years; 97,7% identified as female) meditated a mean of 10.02 minutes daily. GLMMs showed no significant influence of cycle phases or health ratings on meditation duration, whereas motivation did (b = 0.02, 95% CI [-.004, .040], p = .018). Post-intervention self-compassion (p = .005, r = .40), mindfulness (p = .023, r = .31) and self-efficacy (p = .001, r = .57) were higher than baseline. Longer meditation correlated with less negative premenstrual symptoms (τ  <sub>β</sub>  = −.25, p = .014) and higher self-compassion (τ  <sub>β</sub>  = .19, p = .042). LMMs showed no significant association between cycle phases and effort.  <bold>Conclusions</bold>  Adoption of a new positive behavior might not be affected by cycle phases as expected. App-based meditation appears to enhance self-compassion, mindfulness and self-efficacy. Relatively longer meditation duration could reduce premenstrual symptoms. Future research on cycle-effects should consider PMS/PMDD.  </p>

[PMID: ] The art of permeability: A computational distinction between depersonalisation and advanced meditation

<p>First-person reports reveal a surprising convergence between symptoms of depersonalisation disorder (DPD) and certain forms of advanced meditation (AM). In both, individuals describe a fading, and even disappearance, of the ordinarily stable sense of being an embodied self. Yet the experiential tone and functional consequences strikingly differ. In DPD this alteration is endured as distressing alienation, while in AM it is deliberately cultivated towards psychological transformation and flourishing. We propose that this difference stems from opposite configurations of interoceptive permeability along the same computational axis. In DPD, defensive attenuation of ascending interoceptive precision limits the influence of bodily evidence on higher-level inference and stabilises a detached self-model. In AM, sustained practice increases the influence of bodily evidence on higher-level inference, allowing interoceptive signals to reshape and at times loosen the processes that sustain the sense of self. We account for meditation-related depersonalisation, a documented adverse effect of contemplative practice, by emphasising that increasing interoceptive precision without the regulatory conditions that render it tolerable reproduces the configuration that anchors the DPD pole. Finally, we outline the insula-prefrontal axis as a plausible substrate for this regulation. Clinical neuroscience has not characterised the regime in which bodily evidence constrains the self-model without recruiting defensive withdrawal, and advanced meditation provides a foundation from which to address this gap.</p>

[PMID: 42716834] Toward an interdisciplinary mind science of advanced meditation.

Meditation research is increasingly moving beyond mindfulness and other early-stage practices toward advanced meditation, encompassing states, stages, and endpoints that progressively unfold with increasing expertise through meditative development. This emerging subfield consists of interdisciplinary research that brings advanced meditation within reach of systematic scientific investigation. Here we articulate this research subfield, including its constitutive conceptual and methodological components and interrelated translational domains: philosophical and theoretical foundations, computational phenomenology, cognitive neuroscience, epidemiology and public health, clinical and well-being outcomes, and relations with psychedelic experiences. This subfield represents an important development for meditation research and the mind sciences more broadly, with implications for health and well-being and for empirical understanding of the human mind.

[PMID: 42770027] Body image phenomenology in reports of psychedelic and meditative experiences: main themes and associations with body attitudes.

Psychedelics and meditation can facilitate transformative experiences with acute changes in sensory processing and self-perception, but research about their relation to narrative and affective-attitudinal body image remains limited. We examined body image themes in psychedelic and meditative experience narratives, compared these between experience types, and examined their associations with body attitudes. We qualitatively analyzed retrospective reports of participants' most personally meaningful psychedelic and meditation experiences (psychedelics: <i>N</i> = 90; meditation: <i>N</i> = 48). Using regression models, we examined associations between identified themes, current body appreciation, and perceived changes in body dissatisfaction, body appreciation, and body connection, and whether individuals with eating disorder traits reported distinctive themes or body attitude changes. Body image-related narratives most commonly involved reduced sense of bodily self and heightened sensory sensitivity. Psychedelic reports more frequently described disembodiment, the loss of body as an anchor, while meditation reports more often involved boundary dissolution while maintaining embodied presence. 3,4-Methylenedioxymethamphetamine (MDMA) reports described heightened embodiment more often than classical psychedelic reports. Both heightened embodiment and disembodiment narratives were associated with perceived improvements in body dissatisfaction and body connection. Positive affective-attitudinal body image themes were associated with perceived improvements in body connection. Participants with current eating disorder traits, including histories of eating disorders, more frequently reported positive affective-attitudinal body image themes and greater perceived reductions in body dissatisfaction. These findings suggest that psychedelic and meditative body image phenomenology, particularly embodiment-related, can be perceived as meaningful for subjective body attitudes.

[PMID: 42735902] Adverse Experiences in Brief Meditation Practices: Randomized Controlled Trial.

<h4>Background</h4>Meditation has become increasingly popular in recent decades. However, relatively little remains known about the prevalence of and risk factors for adverse experiences related to a single meditation practice.<h4>Objective</h4>The objective of our study was to examine adverse experiences associated with 3 brief, digitally delivered meditation practices (mindfulness, self-compassion, and gratitude) relative to using the internet as usual, as well as to investigate whether preintervention characteristics could predict such outcomes.<h4>Methods</h4>In a secondary analysis of a randomized controlled trial using samples that were representative of the US and UK adult populations with regard to ethnicity, sex, and age, we examined adverse experiences associated with 3 brief (ie, 5 or 10 minutes) meditation practices (ie, mindfulness, self-compassion, and gratitude) relative to using the internet as usual. We also investigated the potential of using preintervention characteristics to predict such outcomes.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 5049 participants completed all preintervention measures and were randomly assigned to meditation or control conditions. Across the sample, 4.1% (204/4925) of participants reported having a distressing experience during the intervention, and 7.1% (348/4908) of participants experienced an increase in negative affect from before to after the intervention. The results showed that participants who were randomized to a brief meditation intervention were no more likely to report a distressing experience than those who were randomized to use the internet as usual (odds ratio [OR] 1.05, 95% CI 0.76-1.47; P=.76). The results also showed that participants who were randomized to a brief meditation intervention were less likely to report clinically relevant increases in negative affect relative to using the internet as usual (OR 0.63, 95% CI 0.50-0.80; P<.001). Notably, participants in the 10-minute condition had a significantly higher likelihood of reporting a distressing experience than those in the 5-minute condition (OR 1.42, 95% CI 1.07-1.89; P=.02). Preintervention characteristics showed acceptable discrimination ability to predict a distressing experience (area under the curve=0.73) and slightly lower ability to predict increased negative affect (area under the curve=0.67).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Taken together, we found that the brief, digitally delivered meditation practices tested in this study carry risks of adverse experiences that are comparable to or lower than those of typical activities on the internet; 10-minute condition was more likely to result in distressing experiences than 5-minute condition; and adverse responses to a brief meditation practice can, at least to a certain degree, be predicted using preintervention characteristics.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Open Science Framework 94HKS; https://osf.io/94hks/overview.

[PMID: 42724159] Heartfulness Meditation-induced State and Trait Effects on Long-term, Short-term and Non-meditators Using Connectivity Analysis: An EEG Study on Prefrontal Cortex.

<h4>Background</h4>Heartfulness meditation (HM) is a heart-based meditation practice that features a unique yogic transmission that involves transfer of subtle energy from a certified, experienced meditator to an aspirant.<h4>Purpose</h4>This study was conducted on HM practice to assess the state (momentary changes during meditation result in temporary physiological and neurological shifts) and trait (long-term personality changes developed with experience) effects on the brain using electroencephalogram (EEG) signal analysis.<h4>Methods</h4>Thirty-four participants were recruited into three groups (long-term meditators [LTM], short-term meditators [STM] and non-meditators). EEG signals from the prefrontal cortex were recorded across four states: baseline, meditation, transmission and post-transmission. Functional connectivity and graph theory measures were calculated for each frequency band (delta, theta, alpha and beta). Within-group and between-group analyses were performed to quantify HM-induced state and trait effects. Significance level was tested through statistical analysis.<h4>Results</h4>The results show improved coordination among brain networks, increased global and local efficiency, representing a positive state effect. While performing between-group analysis, significant trait changes were observed in the LTM group over frequency bands (delta, theta and alpha) of the EEG signal. These findings suggest that the altered EEG resulting from meditation reflects neurophysiological changes and functional integration of the brain network.<h4>Conclusion</h4>The positive state and trait effects were observed for HM practice in the prefrontal cortex of the brain. Furthermore, the enhanced connectivity in EEG frequency bands may lead to significant neuroplastic changes. This finding provides insights into employing functional connectivity parameters as measures for quantifying meditation quality.

[PMID: ] Classroom Group Meditation and Frontal EEG Power-Envelope Networks: A Large Scale Hyperscanning Study

<p>Portable electroencephalography (EEG) allows neural activity to be recorded from whole classrooms, but correlated activity across students can arise from shared instructions, parallel changes in arousal or common artefact rather than interpersonal coupling, and dyads within one class are not independent observations. In this exploratory analysis, single-channel frontal EEG was recorded during eyes-closed rest and guided group meditation in twelve classroom sessions at four schools (194 students; 9–23 per session). For each session and band (alpha, theta, delta, beta), sparse partial-correlation networks among participants were estimated from 140 consecutive 2-s band-power values with the Graphical Lasso, using one condition-blind penalty for both conditions. The primary outcome, positive network strength, was compared between conditions with the session as the unit of inference, using exact sign-flip tests and false-discovery-rate control across bands. No band differed between meditation and rest (alpha: mean session difference = 0.00006, 95% CI [−0.00083, 0.00096], raw p = .887, q = .939). No band differed after calibration against circular-shift surrogates or when one penalty was applied to all sessions, and alpha stayed null when any one session was removed. Along the penalty path, 9 of the 12 sessions changed the sign of their own alpha difference. A tuning-free mean pairwise correlation, calibrated against circular-shift surrogates, also showed no condition difference in any band. These data do not show that brief group meditation changes interparticipant EEG organisation in classrooms, and they indicate that per-session penalised network summaries need penalty-path and tuning-free checks before they are interpreted.</p>

[PMID: 42729419] Mind-wandering dynamics during individual vs. group mindfulness meditation.

<h4>Introduction</h4>Although meditation practice encompasses both intrapersonal and interpersonal dimensions, the differential effects of individual versus group meditation settings (IS vs. GS) remain largely unexplored. This study aimed to reduce this gap by comparing the effects of a mindfulness meditation session performed in the two settings, focusing in particular on mind-wandering (MW) dynamics.<h4>Methods</h4>Expert meditators (<i>n</i> = 30) performed two 30-min breath-focused mindfulness meditation sessions, one in IS and one in GS. During the meditations, participants reported the occurrence of MW via button presses. After each meditation, participants completed questionnaires assessing state mindfulness, mood, and meditation-related experiences. Trait-like questionnaires were also collected.<h4>Results</h4>Across the two meditation settings, the average number of MW self-reports was similar, but their temporal course was significantly different: the rate of MW reports per time remained almost constant over time in IS, whereas it slightly increased in GS. Moreover, lower scores were observed in GS than in IS for the mood states of tension-anxiety and fatigue-inertia.<h4>Discussion</h4>The present study showed that the temporal dynamics of MW during mindfulness meditation differed depending on the meditation setting. The study also showed that, on average, meditating in a group allowed expert mindfulness practitioners to feel less tired/inert and less tense/anxious at the end of the session compared with meditating individually. Although preliminary, these findings hint at why complementing individual mindfulness practice with group sessions may be useful, for example, when practitioners experience difficulties maintaining a regular practice.

[PMID: 42779144] Recommending Meditation in Complex PTSD: Clinical Considerations.

<h4>Objective</h4>Posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) guidelines are often applied to complex PTSD (CPTSD), despite CPTSD's distinct symptomatology. Core CPTSD symptoms, including affect dysregulation, negative self-concept, and relational disruption, may substantially alter treatment needs and safety considerations for mindfulness-based interventions. This article discusses clinical considerations for recommending meditation more safely in CPTSD populations.<h4>Methods</h4>Targeted review of the literature on CPTSD screening instruments, meditation-related adverse effects, dissociation, and informed-consent practices in trauma-sensitive care.<h4>Results</h4>CPTSD is characterized by elevated dissociative symptoms and is commonly associated with histories of childhood adversity; both dissociation and childhood adversity have been independently associated with meditation-related adverse events. Patients with CPTSD may also have self-blame and shame vulnerabilities that predispose them to interpret treatment failure as evidence of personal defectiveness. Standard PTSD screening tools do not capture CPTSD-specific symptomatology, which may limit clinicians' ability to identify patients who would benefit from enhanced informed-consent discussions regarding potential meditation-related adverse effects.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Screening with validated instruments, trauma-informed consent conversations, and evidence-informed technique selection represent low-cost, high-impact practice opportunities. As CPTSD moves toward broader formal diagnostic recognition, clinical recommendations should reflect corresponding diagnostic specificity.

[PMID: 42748179] Assessing the effect of Loving-Kindness Meditation on pain and social well-being using a randomized controlled trial design.

Loving-Kindness Meditation (LKM), a contemplative practice that aims to promote social well-being, has also been linked to lower self-reported pain symptoms. However, it remains unknown whether LKM can reduce pain through having a positive impact on social well-being, as well as whether LKM can improve self-reported acute pain and objectively measured acute pain tolerance among a general, healthy population. In this study (ClinicalTrials.gov; NCT04632875), participants (n = 69) completed either a 4-week online LKM training or an active control meditation training (Progressive Muscle Relaxation; PMR), followed by a novel, remote pain induction task: the wall sit test administered over video conference. This study used a two-arm, parallel-group randomized controlled trial design with stratified block randomization and a double-blind baseline session. We hypothesized that, relative to the PMR active control, LKM would improve social well-being, reduce subjective pain reports, and improve acute pain tolerance. Contrary to our hypotheses, the PMR active control training increased perceived social support relative to LKM (p = .028), and neither meditation decreased subjective pain or increased acute pain tolerance over time. Our data were collected during the unique circumstance of the early COVID-19 pandemic, and it is important to interpret our results with consideration of widespread restrictions on in-person social contact and elevated stress during this period. This context may have constrained participants' opportunities for social connection and influenced reports of social support and pain. Nevertheless, our findings add to the current understanding of how meditation, social wellness, and pain may be related.

[PMID: ] Neurospirituality Reappraised: Brain Mechanisms of Transcendent Experience and Beyond

<p>Background:Subjective experiences featuring altered self-awareness, temporal perception, or spatial boundaries—termed Non-Ordinary Experiences (NOEs)—occur across clinical neurology, psychiatry, and psychedelic medicine, including in insular ecstatic seizures, meditation, breathwork, hypnosis, and psychedelic therapy. <h4>Objective:</h4> To synthesize neurobiological and clinical literature on NOEs for neurologists by presenting three heuristic frameworks: 1.A six-dimension phenomenological taxonomy; 2.A tri-level descriptive taxonomy (computational "software", neuromodulatory-autonomic "firmware", and network "hardware"); 3.The Trigger–Dynamic Psyche–4E Appraisal–Outcome Model, which maps how acute neurodynamic shifts resolve into adaptive growth or clinical distress without reducing existential meaning to neural activity. <h4>Methods:</h4> Narrative synthesis of systematic reviews, coordinate-based meta-analyses, and key theoretical frameworks sourced via PubMed/MEDLINE. <h4>Results:</h4> Conflating spirituality, religion, and NOEs causes construct contamination in neuroimaging, compounded by unvalidated single-item measures and non-independent cohorts. An fMRI meta-analysis of meditation (34 studies, N=700) reveals aggregate recruitment of the right rolandic operculum, left insula, supplementary motor area, and hippocampus, arguing against a singular "spiritual center". First-person reports are mapped using a six-dimension schema (experiential content, temporal dynamics, spatial dynamics, volitional dynamics/agency, ownership/embodiment, cognitive stance). Mechanistically, states are organized across three bidirectionally coupled levels: computational inference ("software"), neuromodulatory-autonomic state-setting ("firmware"), and large-scale networks ("hardware"). Five entry routes (meditation, psychedelics, breathwork, hypnosis, focal neurological events) are hypothesized to share shifts in Bayesian precision weighting and network decoupling within the master Trigger–Dynamic Psyche–4E Appraisal–Outcome Model. Crucially, long-term outcomes (integration vs. distress) are not dictated by acute brain states alone, but are mediated by contextual filtering through the 4E Appraisal Prism (embodied, embedded, enacted, extended dimensions). <h4>Conclusions:</h4> Neurospirituality bridges network neuroscience, clinical neurology, and person-centered care. Clarifying biological substrates does not resolve existential validity. Clinicians must maintain a strict distinction between biological mechanisms, subjective experiences, and personal meanings (the Epistemological Invariant) to avoid pathologizing adaptive states or romanticizing neurological disease. Key terms: Spirituality; Neuroimaging; Meditation; Hallucinogens; Epilepsy.</p>

[PMID: ] Spirituality Matters in Deconstructive Meditation: Comparing Secular and Buddhist-Based Mechanisms of Change in Depressive Vulnerability

<p>This exploratory study extends a previously implemented randomized protocol to examine whether incorporating Buddhist-based spiritual and philosophical components into deconstructive meditation is associated with different patterns of psychological change. We compared secular deconstructive meditation (S-SI; n = 49), Buddhist-based deconstructive meditation (B-SI; n = 53), and a waitlist control condition (WL; n = 50) on depression-related outcomes (dysfunctional attitudes, identity threat, and depressive symptoms) and selflessness-related processes (connectedness to humanity, connectedness to nature, and cognitive decentering). Participants completed pre- and post-intervention assessments, and changes across conditions were examined using repeated-measures MANOVA. Within-subject mediation analyses further explored whether changes in selflessness-related processes were associated with changes in depression-related outcomes. Both active conditions showed greater reductions in depressive symptoms than the waitlist condition. Compared with S-SI, B-SI showed greater reductions in dysfunctional attitudes and identity threat, as well as larger increases in connectedness to humanity, connectedness to nature, and cognitive decentering. Within B-SI, changes in cognitive decentering and connectedness to nature showed significant indirect effects on changes in dysfunctional attitudes, whereas changes in connectedness to humanity showed significant indirect effects on changes in identity threat in both active conditions. None of the examined processes showed significant indirect effects on changes in depressive symptoms in either active condition. These findings suggest that the Buddhist-based condition may engage a broader range of self-related processes associated with depressive vulnerability. [PMID: ] Neurospirituality Reappraised: Brain Mechanisms of Transcendent Experience and Beyond

<p>Background:Subjective experiences featuring altered self-awareness, temporal perception, or spatial boundaries—termed Non-Ordinary Experiences (NOEs)—occur across clinical neurology, psychiatry, and psychedelic medicine, including in insular ecstatic seizures, meditation, breathwork, hypnosis, and psychedelic therapy. <h4>Objective:</h4> To synthesize neurobiological and clinical literature on NOEs for neurologists by presenting three heuristic frameworks: 1.A six-dimension phenomenological taxonomy; 2.A tri-level descriptive taxonomy (computational "software", neuromodulatory-autonomic "firmware", and network "hardware"); 3.The Trigger–Dynamic Psyche–4E Appraisal–Outcome Model, which maps how acute neurodynamic shifts resolve into adaptive growth or clinical distress without reducing existential meaning to neural activity. <h4>Methods:</h4> Narrative synthesis of systematic reviews, coordinate-based meta-analyses, and key theoretical frameworks sourced via PubMed/MEDLINE. <h4>Results:</h4> Conflating spirituality, religion, and NOEs causes construct contamination in neuroimaging, compounded by unvalidated single-item measures and non-independent cohorts. Crucially, long-term outcomes (integration vs. distress) are not dictated by acute brain states alone, but are mediated by contextual filtering through the 4E Appraisal Prism (embodied, embedded, enacted, extended dimensions). <h4>Conclusions:</h4> Neurospirituality bridges network neuroscience, clinical neurology, and person-centered care. Clarifying biological substrates does not resolve existential validity. Clinicians must maintain a strict distinction between biological mechanisms, subjective experiences, and personal meanings (the Epistemological Invariant) to avoid pathologizing adaptive states or romanticizing neurological disease. Key terms: Spirituality; Neuroimaging; Meditation; Hallucinogens; Epilepsy.</p>

[PMID: ] Comparing the temporal dynamics of top-down mindfulness and bottom-up reward systems in optimizing cognitive functioning

<p>Optimizing human cognitive functioning requires a delicate balance between top-down self-regulation and bottom-up motivational reinforcement. However, the precise temporal trade-offs between these opposing trajectories (specifically, immediate reward-driven gains versus gradual mindfulness-based structural adaptation) remain poorly understood. Using a mixed longitudinal design, we assessed cognitive and psychological outcomes using the Mindful Attention Awareness Scale, Perceived Stress Scale, Stroop task (executive control), N-back task (working memory), and Rapid Visual Presentation task (sustained attention). Results showed that mindfulness meditation significantly improved working memory (in an N-back task). Interestingly, the Stroop task showed declined accuracy, but with faster reaction times, suggesting a speed-accuracy trade-off rather than genuine improvement for executive control. Moreover, while mindfulness did lead to an increase in the Mindful Attention Awareness Scale, it did not decrease perceived stress. The result patterns for the reward condition differed from mindfulness meditation. Explicit reward resulted in faster reaction times on the Stroop task, but these were accompanied by reduced accuracy, suggesting a speed–accuracy trade-off rather than genuine cognitive improvement for executive control (in line with mindfulness meditation). Reward did not lead to any significant changes over time in other cognitive tasks. Thus, the improvements in working memory over time were specific to mindfulness meditation and not shown for reward-based manipulations. Overall, these findings highlight the importance of distinguishing genuine cognitive improvements from changes in response strategies.</p>

[PMID: 42821399] Effects of brief mindfulness and loving-kindness meditation on associative memory for sad events: A registered report.

Negative emotion can enhance memory for emotional items while impairing the associative binding and coherence of episodic events. We tested whether a brief combined mindfulness and loving-kindness meditation induction could attenuate sadness-related memory costs for multielement social events. Following preregistration, 113 young adults completed a 20-min mindfulness/loving-kindness meditation induction or matched active control, and 106 were retained for analysis after preregistered exclusions. Participants encoded events composed of a sad or neutral face, an object, and a scene, then completed a four-alternative forced-choice associative memory test and emotional ratings for faces and events. Sad events were remembered less accurately and less coherently than neutral events. However, the mindfulness/loving-kindness meditation induction did not reliably attenuate these memory costs. Bayesian analyses favored the null for associative memory accuracy and remained inconclusive for memory coherence. The intervention also did not reliably modulate emotional responses to sad versus neutral material. Emotional ratings revealed a dissociation between stimulus levels: Sad faces showed the aversive profile, whereas ratings of events were more mixed, suggesting that contextualized social sadness may carry a more complex meaning than a simple aversive cue. These findings identify boundary conditions for brief contemplative inductions and suggest that memory coherence may be an informative end point for future work on emotional memory integration. (PsycInfo Database Record (c) 2026 APA, all rights reserved).

[PMID: 42683308] Meditation styles are highly discriminable from EEG at the subject level with limited generalization across the population: a machine-learning study.

Meditation has been associated with improvements in attention, emotional regulation, and mental wellbeing, motivating increasing interest in objective methods for assessing meditative states. In this study, we investigate whether EEG-based machine learning can reliably distinguish between multiple meditation styles and mind-wandering states. EEG data were recorded from experienced meditators performing three meditation styles, Shamatha, Vipassana, and Metta, together with an eyes-closed mind-wandering condition. EEG signals were preprocessed to remove artifacts, and features were extracted from frequency, time-frequency, and time domains. Classification was evaluated using both intra-subject and inter-subject strategies with multiple machine learning classifiers. Results demonstrate high intra-subject classification accuracy across meditation-vs.-mind-wandering and meditation-style comparisons, indicating strongly discriminative subject-specific neural signatures. In contrast, inter-subject performance decreased substantially, particularly for distinguishing meditation styles, suggesting considerable inter-individual variability in meditation-related EEG patterns. Furthermore, temporal analysis revealed that classification performance increases over time in some comparisons, particularly in VIP/MW, suggesting that the neural distinctions between certain meditation states may become more pronounced over time. Additionally, t-SNE visualization showed clear within-subject clustering but increased overlap across subjects, explaining the reduced inter-subject generalization. Overall, these findings highlight the potential of EEG-based machine learning for personalized assessment and monitoring of meditative states while emphasizing the challenges of developing subject-independent meditation classification systems.

[PMID: 42529893] Effects of an Online Meditation Programme on Stress and Fatigue in Critical Care Nurses: A Randomised Controlled Trial.

[PMID: ] Metacognitive Skill Learning as a Mechanism of Advanced Meditation

<p>Despite growing interest in advanced meditation, the cognitive mechanisms underlying these capacities remain poorly understood. This paper develops a cognitive account of advanced meditation as a specialized form of metacognitive skill learning. Drawing on classic accounts of skill acquisition, we propose the automatization of metacognitive control as a mechanism through which meditative skill develops. Through sustained practice, practitioners become less reliant on explicit instructions as metacognitive control becomes increasingly automatic. This shift may allow meditative practice to become more effective, fluent, and less effortful. The resulting metacognitive control is directed toward well-defined phenomenological targets, including advanced absorption states and insight stages. By connecting cognitive mechanisms with neural findings and experiential descriptions, the account offers a testable framework for explaining the development of advanced meditative capacities.</p>

[PMID: ] Meditation Practice and Long-Term Mortality in US Adults: A Doubly Robust Analysis of Pooled National Health Interview Survey Cohorts with a Trial-Informed Bayesian Subgroup Analysis

<h4>ABSTRACT</h4>  <h4>Introduction</h4>  Trial evidence in older adults with hypertension suggests that meditation may lower mortality, but this association has not been tested in a nationally representative study with linked-mortality data. This study estimated meditation–mortality associations among US adults, examined variation by family income, and synthesized trial and survey evidence for the population matched to the trial evidence base. <h4>Methods</h4>  We pooled four National Health Interview Survey cohorts (2002–2017) with mortality follow-up through 2019 (N=90,041; 8,402 [9.3%] reported meditation practice in the past 12 months). Doubly robust survey-weighted Cox models combined stabilized inverse-probability weights with covariate adjustment, overall and by income stratum; 15-year risks were standardized using g-computation, and probabilistic bias analysis addressed exposure misclassification, including a differential-by-income scenario. For non-Hispanic Black adults ≥55 years with hypertension (n=2,786), Bayesian synthesis combined subgroup estimates with a pooled randomized-trial prior. <h4>Results</h4>  Over 818,248 person-years of follow-up, 10,027 all-cause and 3,040 cardiovascular deaths occurred. Doubly robust hazard ratios (HR) were 1.02 (95% CI 0.90–1.16) for all-cause and 0.96 (95% CI 0.79–1.16) for cardiovascular mortality. Among individuals below 200% of the federal poverty level, HRs were 0.85 (All-cause: 95% CI 0.71–1.03) and 0.80 (Cardiovascular mortality: 95% CI 0.57–1.10), compared with 1.10 (95% CI 0.93–1.30) and 1.02 (95% CI 0.80–1.30) among those at/above that threshold (interaction p=0.19 and p=0.62). In the subgroup analysis, posteriors under a 50%-discounted trial prior were 0.84 (All-cause mortality: Pr[HR<1]=0.89) and 0.70 (Cardiovascular mortality: Pr[HR<1]=0.96).  <h4>Conclusions: </h4>: Meditation practice showed no overall association with mortality after doubly robust adjustment. However, estimates suggesting a protective effect were concentrated among adults below 200% of the federal poverty level, and trial-informed synthesis indicated probable benefit in cardiovascular mortality in the high-risk subgroup most comparable to trial participants. These hypothesis-generating findings motivate further study of effect modification using more detailed exposure measurement.

[PMID: 42613121] Synthesising evidence from decades of meditation-based research across populations, interventions, comparisons and outcomes: protocol for a meta-analytic database of randomised controlled trials via the MetaCIH Collaborative.

<h4>Background</h4>The evidence base for meditation-based interventions has been growing significantly in the past decades, as well as other complementary and integrative health interventions. In parallel, meditation practices have become increasingly popular in the public and across various sectors (eg, education, workplace, healthcare). An expert-curated, publicly available data repository of randomised controlled trials with regular updates, coupled with built-in meta-analytic tools, could serve this purpose.<h4>Methods and analysis</h4>This protocol describes the creation of a living review dataset stored in a publicly available platform to support systematic reviews and meta-analyses of randomised controlled trials of meditation-based interventions. There are no restrictions to study population, comparator or outcome. The database will be updated approximately every 6 months. Meta-analyses will be conducted using the 'metapsyTools' package (V.1.0.11 or higher) in R. Cochrane Risk of Bias tool V.2 will be used to evaluate risk of bias. Publication bias will be evaluated via funnel plots and trim-and-fill analyses, Rücker's 'limit meta-analysis method' and the three-parameter selection model.<h4>Ethics and dissemination</h4>Ethics approval is not applicable. The findings from this study will result in a public-facing meta-analytic database, which will be disseminated through open-access repositories, peer-reviewed manuscripts, presentations at relevant national and international conferences, social media and news media platforms and leveraging the study team's existing relationships with stakeholders in the integrative health field and the community advisory board.<h4>Prospero registration number</h4>CRD42024610197.

[PMID: 42213535] Distilling the Neurophenomenological Signatures of Pure Awareness during Transcendental Meditation.

Pure awareness (PA) has been proposed as a form of minimal phenomenal experience, but its neurophenomenological signatures remain poorly characterized. Transcendental meditation (TM) offers a particularly tractable empirical model of PA because its procedure is standardized, its induction is effortless, and it reliably elicits reports of awareness with minimal content. We combined electroencephalography with temporal experience tracing in 33 experienced TM practitioners and their matched controls (performing mental counting). TM practitioners reported significantly greater intensity and temporal variability of PA, independent of years of meditation practice. We then used multivariate classification of theoretically motivated electroencephalography markers spanning temporal entropy, aperiodic activity, complexity, and linear and nonlinear functional connectivity. We observed a double dissociation. When TM was contrasted with counting, temporal entropy and aperiodic dynamics were the strongest discriminators, whereas phase-coherence functional connectivity contributed least. Conversely, when TM was contrasted with its own baseline, low-frequency functional connectivity dominated, whereas temporal entropy contributed minimally. Complementary topographical analyses indicated that these differences were not reducible to a few localized univariate effects, but were better understood as distributed multivariate neural patterns. Finally, TM showed little evidence of carryover into subsequent rest, whereas counting induced more residual change. Together, these findings provide a systematic electrophysiological characterization of PA and support neurophenomenology as a tractable framework for studying minimal phenomenal experience.

[PMID: 42694510] Effects of a Sound-Based Meditative Intervention on Anxiety, Depression, Salivary Cortisol Levels, and Blood Pressure: A Randomized Controlled Trial.

Background Psychological stress contributes to mental health symptoms and cardiovascular risk through pathways involving anxiety, depression, cortisol secretion, autonomic dysregulation, and elevated blood pressure. Sound-based meditation is a nonpharmacological approach that may support stress management, but randomized controlled trial evidence remains limited. Objective The objective was to evaluate whether a structured sound-based meditation intervention was associated with lower post-intervention anxiety symptoms, measured using the Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale-Anxiety subscale (HADS-A), than a remotely delivered music-control condition after four weeks. Depressive symptoms, salivary cortisol concentration, systolic blood pressure, and exploratory diastolic blood pressure were also assessed. Methodology This parallel-group randomized controlled trial enrolled 100 adults aged 21-65 years from meditation centers and surrounding communities in Tamil Nadu, India. Participants were randomly assigned in a 1:1 ratio to a sound-based meditation intervention group (<i>n</i> = 50) or a remotely delivered music-control group (<i>n</i> = 50). The intervention group attended supervised, face-to-face, 60-minute daily sound-based meditation sessions for four weeks, involving recorded 528-Hz audio and acoustic instruments, including singing bowls, gongs, bells, and tingshas. The control group attended supervised 60-minute Google Meet sessions using two standardized, licensed music tracks with scheduled silent intervals. The primary analysis used analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) to compare post-intervention HADS-A scores between groups, adjusted for baseline HADS-A scores. Corresponding ANCOVA models evaluated HADS-D, salivary cortisol, and systolic blood pressure as secondary outcomes; diastolic blood pressure was exploratory. Holm adjustment was applied across the five reported outcomes. Results  Observed baseline characteristics were comparable between groups. In baseline-adjusted ANCOVA analyses, post-intervention HADS-A scores were lower in the intervention group than in the music-control group (adjusted mean difference = -2.55, 95% confidence interval (CI): -3.62 to -1.48; Holm-adjusted <i>P</i> < 0.001; partial <i>η</i> <sup>2</sup> = 0.19). Corresponding adjusted differences favored the intervention group for HADS-D (adjusted mean difference = -2.39, 95% CI: -3.36 to -1.43; Holm-adjusted <i>P</i> < 0.001; partial <i>η</i> <sup>2</sup> = 0.20), salivary cortisol (-2.54 nmol/L, 95% CI: -2.95 to -2.13; Holm-adjusted <i>P</i> < 0.001; partial <i>η</i> <sup>2</sup> = 0.61), systolic blood pressure (-7.62 mmHg, 95% CI: -9.36 to -5.89; Holm-adjusted <i>P</i> < 0.001; partial <i>η</i> <sup>2</sup> = 0.44), and exploratory diastolic blood pressure (-2.22 mmHg, 95% CI: -3.00 to -1.43; Holm-adjusted <i>P</i> < 0.001; partial <i>η</i> <sup>2</sup> = 0.24). No adverse events were reported. Conclusions This randomized controlled trial provides preliminary evidence that participation in a structured sound-based meditation program was associated with greater short-term improvements in anxiety, depression, salivary cortisol, systolic blood pressure, and exploratory diastolic blood pressure compared with a remotely delivered music-control condition. These findings should be confirmed in larger, fully standardized randomized controlled trials with longer follow-up and diverse populations.

[PMID: ] Selective reductions amid global increases in neural information dynamics jointly track self-dissolution in meditation

<p>The sense of self is fundamental to experience, yet its neural basis remains unresolved. Contemplative traditions and empirical evidence suggest it can be voluntarily dissolved while retaining awareness, offering a window onto this question. We combined magnetoencephalography with in-depth phenomenology in long-term meditators (N=45, mean 3,832 practice hours) who reliably modulated self-boundaries across rest, self-maintenance and self-dissolution. Meditation broadly increased entropy and information transfer, yet selective reductions characterized self-dissolution. Reduced posterior-medial high-beta entropy and within-cingulate information transfer differentiated dissolution from maintenance. Information transfer reductions from occipital, cingulate, limbic, and subcortical regions, alongside orbitofrontal entropy stabilization, correlated with dissolution phenomenology. Combined with posterior-medial high-beta power reductions, these correlates explained 52% of dissolution depth variance, yet only information transfer predicted held-out participants. We propose selfhood relies on distributed processes that can be reconfigured in meditation through relaxed priors, gated bottom-up evidence, and stabilized evaluative dynamics, distinct from global disruption during psychedelic ego-dissolution.</p>

[PMID: 42593718] Load-Dependent Modulation of Cognitive Control Following OM Meditation: Time-Resolved EEG Evidence from the N2 Component.

Working memory (WM) depends on dynamic cognitive control processes that adapt to task demands. Although meditation has been associated with enhanced attentional regulation, its effects on load-dependent neural dynamics remain unclear. The present study investigated the effects of one month of OM meditation on behavioral performance and electrophysiological markers of cognitive control during a visual working memory task. Thirty-four healthy adult males were randomly assigned to either an OM meditation group (n = 18) or a non-meditative control group (n = 16). Electroencephalographic activity was recorded while participants performed visual n-back task with three load conditions (0-back, 1-back, and 2-back). Behavioral performance was analyzed using linear mixed-effects models. Event-related potentials (ERPs), including the P1, N1, N2, P3, and late slow wave components, were examined using both component-based and time-resolved analyses. Behaviorally, the meditation group demonstrated greater pre-post improvements in reaction time during the 0-back and 1-back conditions, but not during the 2-back condition. Accuracy remained high across all task conditions. Electrophysiologically, a significant Group × Time × Load interaction was observed for the N2 component, primarily driven by the 1-back condition. Time-resolved permutation testing further revealed a temporally localized N2 modulation under moderate working memory demand, with no reliable effects observed in other ERP components. These findings suggest that OM meditation selectively modulates monitoring-related cognitive control processes in a demand-dependent manner. More broadly, the results support adaptive models of cognitive control and highlight the value of time-resolved electrophysiological approaches for detecting transient neural changes associated with meditation training.

[PMID: ] In-Person Contemplative Dyad Meditation to Increase Mindfulness, Connection, and Well-Being in Young Adults: A Small Randomized Controlled Trial

<p>OBJECTIVES: This study tested whether in-person Contemplative Dyad Meditation (CDM) can help young adults connect and improve well-being. In CDM, two individuals take turns sharing present-moment experiences while the other listens non-judgmentally. CDM was compared to an active social comparison condition, Fast Friends (FF), and a no-intervention group (NI).METHODS: This preregistered, randomized controlled trial enrolled 119 participants aged 18–35 (82% women, 1% non-binary, 17% men). Participants were assigned to CDM, FF, or NI. CDM participants received a meditation training followed by up to six in-person practice sessions over two weeks, whereas FF participants completed up to six structured in-person conversations. Primary intervention-level outcomes—assessed before and after the entire intervention—included self-reported mindfulness, thriving, and loneliness. The primary session-level outcome—assessed before and after each exercise—was closeness. Secondary and exploratory outcomes included intervention-level measures of affect, depression, anxiety, social anxiety, self-compassion, and empathy, as well as session-level measures of affect and heart rate. Analyses were per-protocol, supplemented by a conservative, complete-case intention-to-treat analysis. RESULTS: CDM significantly increased mindfulness compared to both FF and NI, but no effects were found for thriving or loneliness. Lower baseline mindfulness was associated with greater increases in mindfulness following CDM. Both CDM and FF increased momentary closeness from before to after the respective practices. Regarding secondary intervention-level outcomes, CDM increased overall positive affect after the intervention period, whereas no effects emerged for the remaining measures or for FF. However, modest statistical power warrants caution in interpreting null findings. At the session-level, both CDM and FF increased momentary positive affect after the practices, while FF additionally reduced momentary negative affect. Exploratory analyses indicated that CDM, but not FF, was associated with reduced heart rate following the exercises, consistent with reduced emotional arousal. Intention-to-treat analyses showed broadly similar patterns of results, although mindfulness effects no longer survived correction for multiple comparisons.CONCLUSIONS: We provide initial evidence that CDM can increase mindfulness, positive affect, and social connection in young adults, whereas FF showed benefits primarily for social connection. Exploratory analyses suggested distinct physiological correlates across conditions. CDM involved a higher time investment due to the initial meditation training, potentially affecting comparability. Both CDM and FF may be beneficial for improving social connection, while operating through partly different mechanisms. Given the small sample size and lack of long-term follow-up, further research is needed.</p>

[PMID: ] Psychonautic Benchmarking: Towards Standardized Expertise Criteria in Non-Ordinary States Research

<p>This paper addresses a methodological disparity between meditation research and research involving psychoactive substances. Studies of meditation routinely apply established criteria to distinguish expert practitioners from novices, while research with psychoactive substances screens for safety rather than classifying expertise. We analyze current benchmarking practice in meditation research, examine the limitations of participant selection in psychoactive substance research, and propose a multidimensional framework for psychonaut benchmarking that integrates quantitative exposure metrics, functional capacity assessment, phenomenological sophistication, typological profiling, and standardized competency evaluation. [PMID: 42529315] Brief Mindfulness Meditation Videos: Cultural Sensitivity Review by Chinese American Adolescents.

Given the potential of mindfulness meditation to improve adolescent mental health, this preliminary study examines the cultural sensitivity of four brief mindfulness meditation videos through the ecological cultural validity framework. [PMID: ] Mind-manifesting? Examining naturalistic reports from psychedelics, cannabis, meditation, and dreams from the ALTERD mobile application

Altered states of consciousness (ASC) refer to states that differ markedly from normal waking consciousness. ASC may occur spontaneously, such as in dreaming, or through consciousness modifying techniques, from meditation, to cannabis and psychedelics. The etymology behind the term psychedelic, meaning mind-manifesting, is based on the hypothesis that certain substances, including serotonergic hallucinogens, do not merely distort perception, but also afford genuine observation and insight into the nature of mind, a claim that remains empirically contested today. Here, we address this psychedelic hypothesis using 2,242 reports from the Psychedelics, Cannabis, Meditation, and Dreams categories from the ALTERD mobile application. Across categories, we examined semantic similarity, topic structure, visual phenomenology, and alignment with  a priori ASC themes. The Psychedelics category was most semantically similar to Dreams, consistent with a hallucinogenic character, yet it also uniquely coupled with Meditation across ASC themes, including Personal Growth, lending support for the psychedelic hypothesis. Cannabis showed the opposite pattern: similar to Meditation semantically, but not aligned on ASC themes such as Personal Growth. Together, these findings indicate that psychedelics induce a dream-like state, yet support a meditation-like trajectory toward personal growth, whereas cannabis may share phenomenological features with meditation, but lacking a transformative therapeutic character.

[PMID: ] Selective reductions amid global increases in neural information dynamics jointly track self-dissolution in meditation

<p>The sense of self is fundamental to experience, yet its neural basis remains unresolved. Contemplative traditions and empirical evidence suggest it can be voluntarily dissolved while retaining awareness, offering a window onto this question. We combined magnetoencephalography with in-depth phenomenology in long-term meditators (N=45, mean 3,832 practice hours) who reliably modulated self-boundaries across rest, self-maintenance and self-dissolution. Meditation broadly increased entropy and information transfer, yet selective reductions characterized self-dissolution. Reduced posterior-medial high-beta entropy and within-cingulate information transfer differentiated dissolution from maintenance. Information transfer reductions from occipital, cingulate, limbic, and subcortical regions, alongside orbitofrontal entropy stabilization, correlated with dissolution phenomenology. Combined with posterior-medial high-beta power reductions, these correlates explained 52% of dissolution depth variance, yet only information transfer predicted held-out participants. We propose selfhood relies on distributed processes that can be reconfigured in meditation through relaxed priors, gated bottom-up evidence, and stabilized evaluative dynamics, distinct from global disruption during psychedelic ego-dissolution.</p>

[PMID: 42670176] The Neuroscience of Religious and Spiritual Practices: A Systematic Review of Neurotheological Evidence.

<h4>Background</h4>Religious and spiritual practices are among the most ancient and universal human behaviors, yet their neurobiological substrates remain incompletely characterized. Neurotheology-the scientific study of the relationship between spiritual experience and brain function-has produced growing evidence that such practices generate measurable, systematic, and neuroplastic changes in brain structure and function. [PMID: ] Introspection and Difficulties in Documenting Subjective Experiences of Meditation

<p>Introspective research into subjective experiences of meditation is constrained by an inability to document meditative experiences in a manner that addresses their irreducible qualities. Meditators' integration attempts are negatively affected due to this methodological gap. Thus, more responsive documentation methods need to be developed within introspective research. This paper theorizes that documentation failure in introspective research stems from a fundamental linguistic flaw embedded in how pre-linguistic felt-significance relates to downstream documentation methods. During a subjective meditative event, an experience possesses felt-meaning, defined as the direct perception of a quantity of meaning within the event itself. This felt-meaning is pre-linguistic and serves as the raw material out of which expressed meaning—the formal linguistic description—is constructed. The primary cause of documentation difficulty arises when pre-linguistic events are perceived to be devoid of felt-significance. When an experience presents no identifiable pattern from which to construct a linguistic description, generating expressed meaning becomes increasingly difficult, exposing a hard limit of language itself.</p>

[PMID: 42792287] The Effect of Brief Mindfulness Meditation on Attention Networks in University Students with Problematic Short-Video Use.

To examine the effects of brief mindfulness meditation on attention network function in university students with problematic short-video use, participants were screened using the SPAI and an adapted IAT and assigned to an intervention or control group. The intervention group received 4 weeks of brief mindfulness meditation, whereas the control group received no training. Both groups completed the Attention Network Test before and after the intervention. [PMID: 41955348] Distinct Patterns of Directed Brain Connectivity in Focused Attention, Open Monitoring, and Loving Kindness Meditation: An Electroencephalographic Granger Causality Study with Long-term Meditators.

The present study applied spectral Granger causality (GC) analysis to EEG recordings obtained during focused attention meditation (FAM), open monitoring meditation (OMM), and loving kindness meditation (LKM) in highly experienced meditators. The aim of the investigation was to characterize distinct connectivity signatures associated with each meditation style by examining the strength, frequency, and direction of interregional interactions. These differences were expected to specify the neural connectivity profiles of the cognitive and affective state of each meditative practice. Multivariate GC was computed from high-resolution EEG signals recorded from long-term meditators (n = 22) in four conditions: rest, FAM, OMM, and LKM. GC was analyzed in the frequency domain for key electrode clusters (anterior and posterior) in the two hemispheres to compare frequency-specific directed connectivity between rest and each meditation type. Main results demonstrated that each meditation state produced highly specific alterations in directed influences relative to rest. In FAM, there was significant reduction in posterior-to-anterior GC in the alpha and beta bands as well as decreased multispectral interhemispheric frontal GC implying attenuated bottom-up sensory and associative inputs. In OMM, multispectral GC was significantly increased from the left hemisphere to the right posterior cluster implying expanded awareness in the right posterior regions through enhanced top-down modulation by the left hemisphere. The distinctive features of LKM profile were the interhemispheric symmetry, the posterior-anterior bidirectionality, and the specific beta-band engagement, implying a coactivation of systems that support an emotionally balanced stance, equanimity, and prosocial attitude. These novel findings demonstrate that the direction and frequency specificity of information flows provide complementary insights into neural processes underlying distinct meditative states.

Parental anxiety was measured before and after implementation of the protocol using the State-Trait Anxiety Inventory-6 (STAI-6). Pre- and postintervention anxiety scores were statistically analyzed to evaluate the effectiveness of the protocol.<h4>Findings</h4>Implementation of the Parental Anxiety Reduction Protocol resulted in a statistically significant reduction in parental anxiety scores. Postintervention STAI-6 scores were significantly lower than preintervention scores (P < .001), demonstrating that the multimodal perioperative intervention was effective in reducing parental anxiety.<h4>Conclusions</h4>A structured, evidence-based perioperative protocol incorporating parental education, pediatric play therapy, and parental meditation significantly reduced parental anxiety among parents of children undergoing outpatient surgery. Reducing parental anxiety may also help mitigate pediatric perioperative anxiety and contribute to improved perioperative experiences and outcomes for children and their families.

[PMID: 42574777] The effects of meditation-based interventions on sleep quality among adults aged ≥ 55 with mild cognitive impairment: A systematic review and meta-analysis.

Sleep disturbances are common in older adults with MCI, associated with cognitive decline and reduced quality of life. Pharmacological treatment remains common despite safety concerns. Meditation-based interventions may improve sleep and psychological well-being, yet evidence among individuals with MCI remains limited. This systematic review evaluated the effects of MBIs on sleep quality among adults aged ≥55 years with MCI, following PRISMA 2020 guidelines. Five databases were searched through June 2025, identifying RCTs, pilot RCTs, and quasi-experimental studies using validated sleep measures. Seven studies (n = 434) met inclusion criteria, with interventions lasting 4-12 weeks and encompassing mindfulness, mantra, sound meditation, and hybrid programs. Five studies showed significant improvements in subjective sleep quality; one actigraphy-based study reported reduced daytime napping. A random-effects meta-analysis of six studies reporting PSQI outcomes demonstrated a significant overall improvement in sleep quality (Hedges' g = -0.75, 95% CI -1.23 to -0.28), although substantial heterogeneity was observed (I² = 77.9%). Secondary outcomes included reduced anxiety, stress, and depression, and improved attention and memory. Evidence indicated moderate certainty that MBIs improve sleep quality in MCI. These findings suggest MBIs are promising non-pharmacological strategies to enhance subjective sleep quality and psychological well-being in older adults with MCI. Integrating meditation-based strategies could be explored as a potential, supportive approach within gerontological nursing and community programs, but caution is warranted given the limited and heterogeneous evidence base. Larger, multi-site RCTs with standardized protocols, including both objective and subjective sleep assessments, are needed to strengthen generalizability and guide evidence-based practice.
